Game:  The game is a variant of Nim where players take turns removing 1 to 3 items from a single pile, and the player who takes the last item wins.
Strategy:  The winning strategy for this variant of Nim is to always leave a multiple of 4 items for your opponent after your turn. This means that if the pile size is not a multiple of 4 at the start of your turn, you should remove enough items to make it a multiple of 4. If the pile size is already a multiple of 4 at the start of your turn, you cannot force a win if your opponent plays optimally. In this case, you should try to disrupt your opponent's strategy by taking 1, 2, or 3 items, hoping they make a mistake in subsequent turns.

The winning strategy for this variant of Nim is to always leave your opponent with a number of items that is a multiple of 4. This is because if you start your turn with a number of items that is not a multiple of 4, you can always adjust your move to leave a multiple of 4 for your opponent. Specifically, if the pile has n items where n % 4 != 0, remove enough items to make the remaining pile a multiple of 4. If n % 4 == 0, your opponent can force you into a losing position if they play optimally. Therefore, the key is to always aim to leave a multiple of 4 for your opponent after your turn.

Game:  The game is a variant of Nim where players take turns removing 1 to 3 items from a single pile, and the player who takes the last item wins.
Strategy:  The winning strategy involves ensuring that your opponent is always left with a number of items that is a multiple of 4 after your turn. To achieve this, on your first move, if the number of items in the pile is not already a multiple of 4, remove enough items to make it so. For example, if there are 5 items, remove 1 to leave 4. If there are 6 items, remove 2 to leave 4, and so on. On subsequent turns, adjust your move to maintain this pattern, always leaving a multiple of 4 for your opponent. If you start your turn with a multiple of 4, no matter what you do, your opponent can always return the pile to a multiple of 4, so the key is to avoid this situation by controlling the game from the start.

Game:  The game is a variant of Nim where players take turns removing 1 to 3 items from a single pile, and the player who takes the last item wins.
Strategy:  The winning strategy involves ensuring that your opponent is always left with a number of items that is a multiple of 4. To achieve this, on your turn, calculate the remainder of the current pile size when divided by 4. If the remainder is 0, you are in a losing position if both players play optimally. If the remainder is 1, 2, or 3, remove enough items to leave a multiple of 4 for your opponent. Specifically, if the remainder is 1, remove 1 item; if it is 2, remove 2 items; and if it is 3, remove 3 items. This strategy forces your opponent into a position where they cannot win if you continue to play optimally.

If you start your turn with a multiple of 4, your opponent can always adjust their move to leave you with a non-multiple of 4, so the key is to avoid this situation by controlling the game to ensure you always leave a multiple of 4 for your opponent.

Strategy:  The winning strategy involves ensuring that your opponent is always left with a number of items that is a multiple of 4. Start by making the pile a multiple of 4 if possible. If you cannot start this way, adjust your moves to reach this state. For example, if there are 5 items, take 1 to leave 4. If there are 6 items, take 2 to leave 4. If there are 7 items, take 3 to leave 4. If there are 8 items, take 3 to leave 5, and so on. Always aim to leave a multiple of 4 after your turn.

Game:  The game is a variant of Nim where players take turns removing 1 to 3 items from a pile, and the player who takes the last item wins.
Strategy:  The winning strategy involves ensuring that your opponent is always left with a number of items that is a multiple of 4. On your turn, calculate the remainder of the current pile size when divided by 4. If the remainder is 0, you are in a losing position if both players play optimally. If the remainder is not 0, remove enough items to leave a multiple of 4 for your opponent. For example, if there are 5 items, remove 1 to leave 4; if there are 6 items, remove 2 to leave 4; if there are 7 items, remove 3 to leave 4. This strategy ensures that you maintain control and eventually force your opponent into a losing position.
